<body>

<div class="paper">
  <div class="watermark-badge">TEACHER'S ANSWER KEY</div>

  <div class="header">
    <div>
      <h1>ANSWER KEY</h1>
      <p style="margin: 0; font-weight: 600;">Force & Electric Field Vector Analysis</p>
    </div>
  </div>

  <h2>3. Skill Test Solutions</h2>
  
  <!-- PROBLEM 1 -->
  <div class="exercise">
    <p><strong>Problem 1 (Collinear Forces):</strong> Three collinear charges. Charge A (\(+2 \, \mu\text{C}\)) is on the left, charge B (\(-1 \, \mu\text{C}\)) is in the middle, and charge C (\(+4 \, \mu\text{C}\)) is on the right. The distance from A to B is \(3 \, \text{cm}\), and from B to C is \(3 \, \text{cm}\). Determine the magnitude and direction of the total Coulomb force experienced by charge B!</p>
    
    <div class="solution-box">
      <div class="solution-grid">
        <!-- Visual P1 -->
        <div class="solution-vis" style="width: 250px;">
          <svg viewBox="0 0 250 100" width="100%">
            <defs>
              <marker id="arrRed" markerWidth="8" markerHeight="8" refX="7" refY="4" orient="auto"><path d="M0,0 L8,4 L0,8 Z" fill="#dc2626"/></marker>
              <marker id="arrBlu" markerWidth="8" markerHeight="8" refX="7" refY="4" orient="auto"><path d="M0,0 L8,4 L0,8 Z" fill="#2563eb"/></marker>
            </defs>
            <line x1="20" y1="50" x2="230" y2="50" stroke="#ccc" stroke-dasharray="4,4"/>
            <!-- Charges -->
            <circle cx="40" cy="50" r="12" fill="none" stroke="#dc2626" stroke-width="2"/>
            <text x="40" y="54" text-anchor="middle" fill="#dc2626" font-size="10">+</text>
            <text x="40" y="75" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10">A</text>
            
            <circle cx="125" cy="50" r="12" fill="none" stroke="#2563eb" stroke-width="2"/>
            <text x="125" y="54" text-anchor="middle" fill="#2563eb" font-size="10">−</text>
            <text x="125" y="75" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10">B</text>
            
            <circle cx="210" cy="50" r="12" fill="none" stroke="#dc2626" stroke-width="2"/>
            <text x="210" y="54" text-anchor="middle" fill="#dc2626" font-size="10">+</text>
            <text x="210" y="75" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10">C</text>
            
            <!-- Forces -->
            <line x1="110" y1="30" x2="60" y2="30" stroke="#dc2626" stroke-width="2" marker-end="url(#arrRed)"/>
            <text x="85" y="22" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10" fill="#dc2626">F_BA</text>
            
            <line x1="140" y1="30" x2="190" y2="30" stroke="#2563eb" stroke-width="2" marker-end="url(#arrBlu)"/>
            <text x="165" y="22" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10" fill="#2563eb">F_BC</text>
          </svg>
        </div>
        <!-- Math P1 -->
        <div class="solution-math">
          <strong>Step 1: Calculate magnitudes</strong><br>
          \( F_{BA} = 9 \times 10^9 \frac{(2 \times 10^{-6})(1 \times 10^{-6})}{(0.03)^2} = \frac{18 \times 10^{-3}}{9 \times 10^{-4}} = 20 \, \text{N} \) (Left)<br><br>
          \( F_{BC} = 9 \times 10^9 \frac{(1 \times 10^{-6})(4 \times 10^{-6})}{(0.03)^2} = \frac{36 \times 10^{-3}}{9 \times 10^{-4}} = 40 \, \text{N} \) (Right)<br><br>
          <strong>Step 2: Resultant</strong><br>
          \( \Sigma F_B = F_{BC} - F_{BA} = 40 - 20 = \mathbf{20 \, \text{N}} \) <strong>to the Right</strong>.
        </div>
      </div>
    </div>
  </div>

  <!-- PROBLEM 2 -->
  <div class="exercise">
    <p><strong>Problem 2 (Perpendicular Fields):</strong> Point P is located \(3 \, \text{cm}\) exactly above charge Q1 (\(+4 \, \mu\text{C}\)) and \(4 \, \text{cm}\) to the left of charge Q2 (\(-3 \, \mu\text{C}\)). If the constant \( k = 9 \times 10^9 \), calculate the total electric field strength at point P!</p>
    
    <div class="solution-box">
      <div class="solution-grid">
        <!-- Visual P2 -->
        <div class="solution-vis" style="width: 200px;">
          <svg viewBox="0 0 200 150" width="100%">
            <defs>
              <marker id="arrBlk" markerWidth="8" markerHeight="8" refX="7" refY="4" orient="auto"><path d="M0,0 L8,4 L0,8 Z" fill="#000"/></marker>
            </defs>
            <line x1="40" y1="120" x2="40" y2="40" stroke="#ccc" stroke-dasharray="3,3"/>
            <line x1="40" y1="40" x2="160" y2="40" stroke="#ccc" stroke-dasharray="3,3"/>
            
            <circle cx="40" cy="120" r="10" fill="none" stroke="#dc2626" stroke-width="2"/>
            <text x="40" y="124" text-anchor="middle" fill="#dc2626" font-size="10">+</text>
            <text x="20" y="124" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10">Q1</text>
            
            <circle cx="160" cy="40" r="10" fill="none" stroke="#2563eb" stroke-width="2"/>
            <text x="160" y="44" text-anchor="middle" fill="#2563eb" font-size="10">−</text>
            <text x="180" y="44" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10">Q2</text>
            
            <circle cx="40" cy="40" r="3" fill="#000"/>
            <text x="25" y="35" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10">P</text>
            
            <line x1="40" y1="40" x2="40" y2="10" stroke="#000" stroke-width="1.5" marker-end="url(#arrBlk)"/>
            <text x="55" y="20" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10">E1</text>
            
            <line x1="40" y1="40" x2="90" y2="40" stroke="#000" stroke-width="1.5" marker-end="url(#arrBlk)"/>
            <text x="75" y="35" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10">E2</text>
          </svg>
        </div>
        <!-- Math P2 -->
        <div class="solution-math">
          <strong>Step 1: E from each charge</strong><br>
          \( E_1 = 9 \times 10^9 \frac{4 \times 10^{-6}}{(0.03)^2} = 4 \times 10^7 \, \text{N/C} \) (Upwards, away from Q1)<br><br>
          \( E_2 = 9 \times 10^9 \frac{3 \times 10^{-6}}{(0.04)^2} = \frac{27 \times 10^3}{16 \times 10^{-4}} = 1.6875 \times 10^7 \, \text{N/C} \) (Right, towards Q2)<br><br>
          <strong>Step 2: Pythagorean Resultant</strong><br>
          \( E_P = \sqrt{(4 \times 10^7)^2 + (1.6875 \times 10^7)^2} \approx \mathbf{4.34 \times 10^7 \, \text{N/C}} \)
        </div>
      </div>
    </div>
  </div>

  <div class="page-break"></div>

  <!-- PROBLEM 3 -->
  <div class="exercise">
    <p><strong>Problem 3 (Resultant Challenge):</strong> Two attractive electrical forces act on a test charge. The first force is \( 6 \, \text{N} \) pointing North, and the second force is \( 8 \, \text{N} \) pointing East. What is the magnitude of the resultant force experienced by the charge?</p>
    <div class="solution-box">
      <div class="solution-grid">
        <div class="solution-vis" style="width: 180px;">
          <svg viewBox="0 0 150 150" width="100%">
            <defs><marker id="arrB" markerWidth="8" markerHeight="8" refX="7" refY="4" orient="auto"><path d="M0,0 L8,4 L0,8 Z" fill="#000"/></marker></defs>
            <line x1="30" y1="120" x2="30" y2="40" stroke="#000" stroke-width="2" marker-end="url(#arrB)"/>
            <text x="15" y="80" text-anchor="middle" font-size="12">6 N</text>
            <line x1="30" y1="120" x2="110" y2="120" stroke="#000" stroke-width="2" marker-end="url(#arrB)"/>
            <text x="70" y="135" text-anchor="middle" font-size="12">8 N</text>
            <line x1="30" y1="120" x2="105" y2="45" stroke="#dc2626" stroke-width="2" stroke-dasharray="4,2" marker-end="url(#arrRed)"/>
            <text x="80" y="70" text-anchor="middle" font-size="12" fill="#dc2626">R</text>
          </svg>
        </div>
        <div class="solution-math">
          <p>North and East form a \( 90^\circ \) angle. Use Pythagoras:</p>
          \[ R = \sqrt{F_{\text{North}}^2 + F_{\text{East}}^2} \]
          \[ R = \sqrt{6^2 + 8^2} = \sqrt{36 + 64} = \sqrt{100} = \mathbf{10 \, \text{N}} \]
          <p><em>(Matches the 3-4-5 triangle pattern: \( 3(2) \)-div-\( 4(2) \)-div-\( 5(2) \))</em></p>
        </div>
      </div>
    </div>
  </div>

  <!-- PROBLEM 4 -->
  <div class="exercise">
    <p><strong>Problem 4 (Midpoint Field):</strong> Point A is located exactly in the middle between charge Q1 (\(+2 \, \mu\text{C}\)) and charge Q2 (\(-4 \, \mu\text{C}\)), which are separated by a total distance of \(6 \, \text{cm}\). Calculate the total electric field strength and determine its direction at point A!</p>
    <div class="solution-box">
      <div class="solution-grid">
        <div class="solution-vis" style="width: 280px;">
          <svg viewBox="0 0 280 100" width="100%">
            <line x1="30" y1="60" x2="250" y2="60" stroke="#ccc" stroke-dasharray="4,4"/>
            <circle cx="40" cy="60" r="10" fill="none" stroke="#dc2626" stroke-width="2"/>
            <text x="40" y="64" text-anchor="middle" fill="#dc2626" font-size="10">+</text>
            <text x="40" y="85" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10">Q1</text>
            
            <circle cx="240" cy="60" r="10" fill="none" stroke="#2563eb" stroke-width="2"/>
            <text x="240" y="64" text-anchor="middle" fill="#2563eb" font-size="10">−</text>
            <text x="240" y="85" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10">Q2</text>
            
            <circle cx="140" cy="60" r="3" fill="#000"/>
            <text x="140" y="85" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10">Point A</text>
            
            <line x1="140" y1="45" x2="180" y2="45" stroke="#000" stroke-width="1.5" marker-end="url(#arrBlk)"/>
            <text x="160" y="35" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10">E1 (Right)</text>
            
            <line x1="140" y1="20" x2="210" y2="20" stroke="#000" stroke-width="1.5" marker-end="url(#arrBlk)"/>
            <text x="175" y="10" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10">E2 (Right)</text>
          </svg>
        </div>
        <div class="solution-math">
          <strong>Step 1: Distance to A</strong><br>
          \( r_1 = r_2 = 3 \, \text{cm} = 0.03 \, \text{m} \)<br><br>
          <strong>Step 2: Field Vectors</strong><br>
          \( E_1 = 9 \times 10^9 \frac{2 \times 10^{-6}}{0.0009} = 2 \times 10^7 \, \text{N/C} \) (Right, away from +)<br>
          \( E_2 = 9 \times 10^9 \frac{4 \times 10^{-6}}{0.0009} = 4 \times 10^7 \, \text{N/C} \) (Right, towards −)<br><br>
          <strong>Step 3: Resultant</strong><br>
          Both vectors point in the same direction, so we add them:<br>
          \( E_A = E_1 + E_2 = \mathbf{6 \times 10^7 \, \text{N/C}} \) <strong>to the Right (towards Q2)</strong>.
        </div>
      </div>
    </div>
  </div>

  <!-- PROBLEM 5 -->
  <div class="exercise">
    <p><strong>Problem 5 (Perpendicular Forces):</strong> A test charge experiences a Coulomb force of \( 15 \, \text{N} \) to the West and \( 20 \, \text{N} \) to the South due to nearby charges. Determine the magnitude of the resultant force acting on the test charge!</p>
    <div class="solution-box">
      <div class="solution-grid">
        <div class="solution-vis" style="width: 180px;">
          <svg viewBox="0 0 150 150" width="100%">
            <line x1="120" y1="30" x2="40" y2="30" stroke="#000" stroke-width="2" marker-end="url(#arrB)"/>
            <text x="80" y="20" text-anchor="middle" font-size="12">15 N (W)</text>
            <line x1="120" y1="30" x2="120" y2="110" stroke="#000" stroke-width="2" marker-end="url(#arrB)"/>
            <text x="135" y="70" text-anchor="middle" font-size="12">20 N</text>
            <line x1="120" y1="30" x2="45" y2="105" stroke="#dc2626" stroke-width="2" stroke-dasharray="4,2" marker-end="url(#arrRed)"/>
            <text x="70" y="80" text-anchor="middle" font-size="12" fill="#dc2626">R</text>
          </svg>
        </div>
        <div class="solution-math">
          <p>West and South form a \( 90^\circ \) angle. Use Pythagoras:</p>
          \[ R = \sqrt{15^2 + 20^2} \]
          \[ R = \sqrt{225 + 400} \]
          \[ R = \sqrt{625} = \mathbf{25 \, \text{N}} \]
          <p><em>(Matches the 3-4-5 triangle pattern: \( 3(5) \)-div-\( 4(5) \)-div-\( 5(5) \))</em></p>
        </div>
      </div>
    </div>
  </div>

  <div class="footer-note">
    Mr. Alfi's Physics Class, SMA ABBS Surakarta
  </div>

</div>

</body>

<body>

      <div class="options-grid">
        <button class="option-btn" onclick="cek(this, false, 'F = 3+4 adalah 7. Segitiga Pythagoras 3 dan 4 menghasilkan sisi miring 5.')">F = √(3+4) = 7N</button>
        <button class="option-btn" onclick="cek(this, true, 'Luar Biasa! F = √(3² + 4²) = √25 = 5 Newton.', 'lvl4-res')">F = √(3² + 4²) = 5N</button>

<script>
// Fungsi Cek Kuis & Memicu Animasi
function cek(btn, isCorrect, msg, animateIds = '') {
  const card = btn.closest('.quiz-card');
  const levelSection = btn.closest('.level');
  const allBtns = card.querySelectorAll('.option-btn');
  const feedback = card.querySelector('.feedback');

  allBtns.forEach(b => { b.classList.remove('correct', 'wrong'); b.disabled = true; });
  feedback.classList.remove('show', 'correct', 'wrong');

  setTimeout(() => {
    feedback.innerHTML = msg;
    feedback.classList.add('show', isCorrect ? 'correct' : 'wrong');
    
    if (isCorrect) {
      btn.classList.add('correct');
      
      if (animateIds) {
        const ids = animateIds.split(',');
        ids.forEach((id, index) => {
          setTimeout(() => {
            const group = document.getElementById(id);
            if(group) {
              group.classList.add('show');
              group.querySelectorAll('.v-line').forEach(line => line.classList.add('draw'));
            }
          }, index * 200); 
        });
      }

      const currentStep = parseInt(card.getAttribute('data-step'));
      const nextCard = levelSection.querySelector(`.quiz-card[data-step="${currentStep + 1}"]`);
      card.classList.add('completed');
      
      if(nextCard) {
        nextCard.classList.add('active');
        setTimeout(() => nextCard.scrollIntoView({ behavior: 'smooth', block: 'center' }), 600);
      }
    } else {
      btn.classList.add('wrong');
      setTimeout(() => allBtns.forEach(b => b.disabled = false), 800); 
    }
  }, 10);
}

// Fungsi Reset spesifik per Level
function resetLevel(lvlId) {
  const section = document.getElementById(lvlId);
  
  section.querySelectorAll('.v-group').forEach(el => el.classList.remove('show'));
  section.querySelectorAll('.v-line').forEach(el => el.classList.remove('draw'));

  section.querySelectorAll('.quiz-card').forEach((card, index) => {
    card.classList.remove('active', 'completed');
    card.querySelector('.feedback').classList.remove('show', 'correct', 'wrong');
    card.querySelectorAll('.option-btn').forEach(btn => {
      btn.classList.remove('correct', 'wrong');
      btn.disabled = false;
    });
    if(index === 0) card.classList.add('active');
  });
}

// Highlight Navigasi Saat Scroll
window.addEventListener('scroll', () => {
  let current = '';
  document.querySelectorAll('.level').forEach(section => {
    const sectionTop = section.offsetTop;
    if (pageYOffset >= sectionTop - 120) {
      current = section.getAttribute('id');
    }
  });

  document.querySelectorAll('.nav-btn').forEach(a => {
    a.classList.remove('active');
    if (a.getAttribute('href').includes(current)) {
      a.classList.add('active');
    }
  });
});
</script>

</body>
</html>

  <h2 style="margin-top: 1.5rem;">3. Skill Test (Workspace)</h2>
  
  <div class="exercise">
    <p><strong>Problem 1 (Collinear Forces):</strong> Three collinear charges. Charge A (\(+2 \, \mu\text{C}\)) is on the left, charge B (\(-1 \, \mu\text{C}\)) is in the middle, and charge C (\(+4 \, \mu\text{C}\)) is on the right. The distance from A to B is \(3 \, \text{cm}\), and from B to C is \(3 \, \text{cm}\). Determine the magnitude and direction of the total Coulomb force experienced by charge B!</p>
    <div class="workspace workspace-p1"></div>
  </div>

  <div class="exercise">
    <p><strong>Problem 2 (Perpendicular Fields):</strong> Point P is located \(3 \, \text{cm}\) exactly above charge Q1 (\(+4 \, \mu\text{C}\)) and \(4 \, \text{cm}\) to the left of charge Q2 (\(-3 \, \mu\text{C}\)). If the constant \( k = 9 \times 10^9 \), calculate the total electric field strength at point P!</p>
    <div class="workspace workspace-p1"></div>
  </div>

    <p><strong>Problem 3 (Resultant Challenge):</strong> Two attractive electrical forces act on a test charge. The first force is \( 6 \, \text{N} \) pointing North, and the second force is \( 8 \, \text{N} \) pointing East. What is the magnitude of the resultant force experienced by the charge?</p>
    <div class="workspace workspace-p2"></div>
  </div>

  <div class="exercise">
    <p><strong>Problem 4 (Midpoint Field):</strong> Point A is located exactly in the middle between charge Q1 (\(+2 \, \mu\text{C}\)) and charge Q2 (\(-4 \, \mu\text{C}\)), which are separated by a total distance of \(6 \, \text{cm}\). Calculate the total electric field strength and determine its direction at point A!</p>
    <div class="workspace workspace-p2"></div>
  </div>

  <div class="exercise">
    <p><strong>Problem 5 (Perpendicular Forces):</strong> A test charge experiences a Coulomb force of \( 15 \, \text{N} \) to the West and \( 20 \, \text{N} \) to the South due to nearby charges. Determine the magnitude of the resultant force acting on the test charge!</p>
    <div class="workspace workspace-p2"></div>

    text-transform:uppercase;
    letter-spacing:0.05em;
  }
  table.cheat td{
    padding:10px 12px;
    border-bottom:1px solid var(--grid-line);
    vertical-align:top;
  }
  table.cheat td.formula{font-family:'JetBrains Mono',monospace;color:var(--field-blue);font-weight:600;white-space:nowrap;}
  table.cheat tr:nth-child(even) td{background:var(--paper-warm);}

  svg{display:block;width:100%;height:auto;}

  .cta{
    margin-top:60px;
    text-align:center;

    color:#fff;
  }

<body>

<div class="identity-bar">
  <div class="identity">Physics 12 <span>·</span> SMA ABBS Surakarta</div>
  <a class="worksheet-link" href="student-worksheet.html">Open activity sheet →</a>

<div class="wrap">

  <!-- HERO -->
  <section class="hero">
    <div class="eyebrow">Electricity &amp; Magnetism · Chapter Guide</div>
    <h1>Gauss's Law &amp; <em>Electric Potential</em></h1>
    <p class="sub">Two ideas, one story: counting invisible field lines through a surface, and mapping the electric "height" that makes charges want to move. No heavy math — just patterns, pictures, and small steps.</p>

    <svg class="hero-svg" viewBox="0 0 480 300" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg">
      <circle cx="240" cy="150" r="4" fill="#E2792B"/>
      <text x="240" y="128" text-anchor="middle" font-family="JetBrains Mono" font-size="13" fill="#E2792B" font-weight="600">+Q</text>
      <g stroke="#2E6F9E" stroke-width="1.6" fill="none" opacity="0.85">
        <line x1="240" y1="150" x2="240" y2="20"/>
        <line x1="240" y1="150" x2="240" y2="280"/>
        <line x1="240" y1="150" x2="90" y2="150"/>
        <line x1="240" y1="150" x2="390" y2="150"/>
        <line x1="240" y1="150" x2="134" y2="44"/>
        <line x1="240" y1="150" x2="346" y2="44"/>
        <line x1="240" y1="150" x2="134" y2="256"/>
        <line x1="240" y1="150" x2="346" y2="256"/>
      </g>
      <g fill="#2E6F9E">
        <polygon points="240,26 235,38 245,38"/>
        <polygon points="240,274 235,262 245,262"/>
        <polygon points="96,150 108,145 108,155"/>
        <polygon points="384,150 372,145 372,155"/>
        <polygon points="139,50 148,58 138,60"/>
        <polygon points="341,50 332,58 342,60"/>
        <polygon points="139,250 148,242 138,240"/>
        <polygon points="341,250 332,242 342,240"/>
      </g>
      <circle cx="240" cy="150" r="95" fill="none" stroke="#1E8A79" stroke-width="2" stroke-dasharray="6 5"/>
      <text x="240" y="270" text-anchor="middle" font-family="Inter" font-size="12" fill="#1E8A79" font-weight="600">Gaussian surface (imaginary, closed)</text>
    </svg>
    <div class="flux-count">Field lines crossing the dashed surface: <b>8 out</b> — all counted, no matter its exact shape.</div>
  </section>

  <!-- STAGE 1 -->
  <section class="stage">
    <div class="stage-badge">1</div>
    <div class="kicker">Warm-up recap</div>
    <h2>What is an Electric Field?</h2>
    <p class="lead">Before flux and Gauss's Law can make sense, we need one picture firmly in mind: the electric field as a set of arrows filling space around a charge.</p>

    <p>An electric field is a way of describing how a charge affects the space around it, even before anything else is there. Instead of drawing forces on every possible test charge, we simplify life by drawing <strong>field lines</strong> — arrows that show the direction a small positive charge would be pushed at that point.</p>

    <div class="box key-idea">
      <span class="box-label">Three rules for field lines</span>
      1. Lines start on positive charges and end on negative charges (or head off to infinity).<br>
      2. Lines never cross each other.<br>
      3. Where lines are packed close together, the field is <em>stronger</em>. Where they spread out, the field is <em>weaker</em>.
    </div>

    <div class="check">
      <div class="qlabel">Check yourself</div>
      <p>Two diagrams show field lines around a charge. In diagram A, the lines are close together. In diagram B, the same number of lines are spread far apart. Which region has the stronger field?</p>
      <button class="reveal-btn" onclick="this.nextElementSibling.classList.toggle('show')">Show answer</button>
      <div class="answer">Diagram A. Line <em>density</em> (how packed they are) tells you field strength — not how many lines you drew in total.</div>
    </div>
  </section>

  <!-- STAGE 2 -->
  <section class="stage">
    <div class="stage-badge">2</div>
    <div class="kicker">Building the tool</div>
    <h2>Electric Flux — Counting Lines Through a Window</h2>
    <p class="lead">Flux just means: "how much field passes through a surface." Nothing more mysterious than that.</p>

    <div class="box analogy">
      <span class="box-label">Analogy</span>
      Imagine standing at an open window on a windy day. If the window faces straight into the wind, a lot of air rushes through. If you turn the window sideways to the wind, almost no air passes through — even though the wind hasn't changed at all. <strong>Electric flux</strong> works the same way, except it's field lines passing through a surface instead of wind through a window.
    </div>

    <svg viewBox="0 0 480 160" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" style="margin:18px 0;">
      <g stroke="#2E6F9E" stroke-width="1.6" fill="none">
        <line x1="20" y1="40" x2="150" y2="40"/><polygon points="150,40 140,35 140,45" fill="#2E6F9E"/>
        <line x1="20" y1="70" x2="150" y2="70"/><polygon points="150,70 140,65 140,75" fill="#2E6F9E"/>
        <line x1="20" y1="100" x2="150" y2="100"/><polygon points="150,100 140,95 140,105" fill="#2E6F9E"/>
      </g>
      <rect x="150" y="20" width="10" height="100" fill="#E4EEF6" stroke="#1E8A79" stroke-width="2"/>
      <text x="155" y="140" text-anchor="middle" font-family="Inter" font-size="11" fill="#1B2340" font-weight="600">θ = 0° (face-on)</text>
      <text x="155" y="153" text-anchor="middle" font-family="Inter" font-size="10" fill="#1E8A79">maximum flux</text>

      <g stroke="#2E6F9E" stroke-width="1.6" fill="none">
        <line x1="260" y1="40" x2="390" y2="40"/><polygon points="390,40 380,35 380,45" fill="#2E6F9E"/>
        <line x1="260" y1="70" x2="390" y2="70"/><polygon points="390,70 380,65 380,75" fill="#2E6F9E"/>
        <line x1="260" y1="100" x2="390" y2="100"/><polygon points="390,100 380,95 380,105" fill="#2E6F9E"/>
      </g>
      <rect x="373" y="55" width="34" height="10" fill="#E4EEF6" stroke="#1E8A79" stroke-width="2" transform="rotate(0 0 0)"/>
      <text x="390" y="140" text-anchor="middle" font-family="Inter" font-size="11" fill="#1B2340" font-weight="600">θ = 90° (edge-on)</text>
      <text x="390" y="153" text-anchor="middle" font-family="Inter" font-size="10" fill="#C0392B">zero flux</text>
    </svg>

    <div class="formula-card">
      <div class="flabel">General formula</div>
      <div class="feq">Φ = E · A · cos θ</div>
      <div class="fnote">Φ (phi) is flux, E is field strength, A is the surface area, and θ is the angle between the field and the surface's perpendicular ("normal") direction. When the field hits the surface straight-on, θ = 0° and cos θ = 1, so Φ = E·A — the simplest case.</div>
    </div>

    <div class="worked">
      <div class="wtitle">Worked example</div>
      <ol>
        <li>A uniform field of <span class="step-eq">E = 500 N/C</span> passes straight through a flat surface of area <span class="step-eq">A = 2 m²</span>, perpendicular to it (θ = 0°).</li>
        <li>Since θ = 0°, cos θ = 1, so we can use <span class="step-eq">Φ = E × A</span>.</li>
        <li>Φ = 500 × 2 = <span class="step-eq">1000 N·m²/C</span>.</li>
      </ol>
    </div>

    <div class="box mistake">
      <span class="box-label">Common mistake</span>
      Forgetting the cos θ term when the surface is tilted. If a problem doesn't say "perpendicular" or "face-on," check the angle before plugging numbers in.
    </div>

    <div class="check">
      <div class="qlabel">Check yourself</div>
      <p>A flat surface is turned so it's exactly edge-on to a field (θ = 90°). What is the flux through it?</p>
      <button class="reveal-btn" onclick="this.nextElementSibling.classList.toggle('show')">Show answer</button>
      <div class="answer">Zero. Since cos 90° = 0, no field lines actually pass through the surface — they slide along it instead.</div>
    </div>
  </section>

  <!-- STAGE 3 -->
  <section class="stage">
    <div class="stage-badge">3</div>
    <div class="kicker">The big idea</div>
    <h2>Gauss's Law</h2>
    <p class="lead">Gauss's Law is one simple sentence: the total flux out of any <em>closed</em> surface only depends on the charge trapped inside it.</p>

    <div class="box analogy">
      <span class="box-label">Analogy</span>
      Picture a closed fishing net wrapped all the way around part of a lake. It doesn't matter if the net is shaped like a ball, a box, or a lumpy potato — only the fish actually <em>inside</em> the net get caught. Charge outside the surface contributes nothing to the total flux, no matter how close it is.
    </div>

    <div class="formula-card">
      <div class="flabel">Gauss's Law</div>
      <div class="feq">Φ<sub style="font-size:13px;">total</sub> = Q<sub style="font-size:13px;">enc</sub> / ε₀</div>
      <div class="fnote">Φ<sub>total</sub> is the total flux through a closed surface, Q<sub>enc</sub> is the total charge enclosed inside that surface, and ε₀ (epsilon-naught) is a fixed constant, ε₀ ≈ 8.85 × 10⁻¹² C²/(N·m²).</div>
    </div>

    <div class="box key-idea">
      <span class="box-label">Key idea</span>
      The <em>shape</em> of the closed surface (called a "Gaussian surface") never matters. We are always free to imagine any convenient closed shape — usually a sphere, cylinder, or box — because Gauss's Law only cares about the charge trapped inside.
    </div>

    <div class="check">
      <div class="qlabel">Check yourself</div>
      <p>If you double the amount of charge enclosed inside a Gaussian surface, what happens to the total flux through that surface?</p>
      <button class="reveal-btn" onclick="this.nextElementSibling.classList.toggle('show')">Show answer</button>
      <div class="answer">It also doubles. Φ<sub>total</sub> = Q<sub>enc</sub>/ε₀ is a direct (linear) relationship — flux scales exactly with enclosed charge.</div>
    </div>
  </section>

  <!-- STAGE 4 -->
  <section class="stage">
    <div class="stage-badge">4</div>
    <div class="kicker">Putting it to work</div>
    <h2>Using Gauss's Law: Field of a Point Charge</h2>
    <p class="lead">Gauss's Law becomes genuinely useful when we pick a Gaussian surface with a lot of symmetry. For a single point charge, that's a sphere.</p>

    <div class="worked">
      <div class="wtitle">Step-by-step reasoning</div>
      <ol>
        <li>Draw an imaginary sphere of radius <span class="step-eq">r</span> centered on the point charge Q.</li>
        <li>By symmetry, the field E has the same strength everywhere on this sphere, and always points straight outward — perpendicular to the surface (θ = 0°, so cos θ = 1).</li>
        <li>The surface area of a sphere is <span class="step-eq">A = 4πr²</span>.</li>
        <li>So the total flux is simply <span class="step-eq">Φ = E × 4πr²</span>.</li>
        <li>Gauss's Law says this must equal <span class="step-eq">Q/ε₀</span>. Setting them equal: E × 4πr² = Q/ε₀.</li>
        <li>Solving for E: <span class="step-eq">E = Q / (4πε₀r²)</span>.</li>
      </ol>
    </div>

    <div class="formula-card">
      <div class="flabel">Field of a point charge</div>
      <div class="feq">E = kQ / r²  &nbsp; where &nbsp; k = 1/(4πε₀) ≈ 9 × 10⁹ N·m²/C²</div>
      <div class="fnote">This is the same formula you may already know from Coulomb's Law — Gauss's Law simply gives us another route to the same answer, and a much faster one for symmetric shapes.</div>
    </div>

    <div class="worked">
      <div class="wtitle">Worked example</div>
      <ol>
        <li>Find the field at r = 0.3 m from a point charge Q = 2 × 10⁻⁶ C.</li>
        <li>E = kQ/r² = (9×10⁹ × 2×10⁻⁶) / (0.3)²</li>
        <li>Numerator: 9×10⁹ × 2×10⁻⁶ = <span class="step-eq">1.8 × 10⁴</span></li>
        <li>Denominator: 0.3² = <span class="step-eq">0.09</span></li>
        <li>E = 1.8×10⁴ / 0.09 = <span class="step-eq">2 × 10⁵ N/C</span></li>
      </ol>
    </div>

    <div class="check">
      <div class="qlabel">Check yourself</div>
      <p>If you move to a point twice as far away from the same charge (r doubles), what happens to E?</p>
      <button class="reveal-btn" onclick="this.nextElementSibling.classList.toggle('show')">Show answer</button>
      <div class="answer">E becomes 4 times smaller. Because E ∝ 1/r², doubling r divides E by 2² = 4.</div>
    </div>
  </section>

  <!-- STAGE 5 -->
  <section class="stage">
    <div class="stage-badge">5</div>
    <div class="kicker">Changing viewpoint</div>
    <h2>From Field to Potential: Electric "Height"</h2>
    <p class="lead">Fields tell us about force. Potential tells us about energy — and energy is often easier to work with because it has no direction to track.</p>

    <div class="box analogy">
      <span class="box-label">Analogy</span>
      Think about a ball sitting on a hill. The higher up the hill, the more gravitational potential energy it has. Roll it, and that stored energy turns into motion. Electric charges behave the same way inside a field: a charge at a location with more electric potential energy will "roll" toward lower potential energy if it's free to move.
    </div>

    <p>Just like we divide gravitational PE by mass to get a property of the location alone (height), we divide electric potential energy by charge to get <strong>electric potential</strong> — a property of the location in the field, independent of whatever charge happens to be sitting there.</p>

    <div class="formula-card">
      <div class="flabel">Definition of electric potential</div>
      <div class="feq">V = PE / q</div>
      <div class="fnote">V is measured in volts (V), where 1 volt = 1 joule per coulomb. Potential belongs to a <em>point in space</em>, the same way elevation belongs to a spot on a map — not to whichever hiker happens to be standing there.</div>
    </div>
  </section>

  <!-- STAGE 6 -->
  <section class="stage">
    <div class="stage-badge">6</div>
    <div class="kicker">A useful formula</div>
    <h2>Potential of a Point Charge</h2>
    <p class="lead">Just like the field, the potential around a point charge follows a clean formula — but this one is simpler, because potential has no direction.</p>

    <div class="formula-card">
      <div class="flabel">Potential of a point charge</div>
      <div class="feq">V = kQ / r</div>
      <div class="fnote">Notice E ∝ 1/r² but V ∝ 1/r — potential falls off more gently with distance. And because V is a scalar (just a number, no direction), adding up the potential from several charges is simple addition — no angles required.</div>
    </div>

    <div class="worked">
      <div class="wtitle">Worked example</div>
      <ol>
        <li>Find V at r = 0.5 m from a charge Q = 3 × 10⁻⁶ C.</li>
        <li>V = kQ/r = (9×10⁹ × 3×10⁻⁶) / 0.5</li>
        <li>Numerator: 9×10⁹ × 3×10⁻⁶ = <span class="step-eq">2.7 × 10⁴</span></li>
        <li>V = 2.7×10⁴ / 0.5 = <span class="step-eq">5.4 × 10⁴ V</span></li>
      </ol>
    </div>

    <div class="check">
      <div class="qlabel">Check yourself</div>
      <p>Two point charges of equal size sit at different distances from you. Which one contributes more to the potential you experience — the near one or the far one?</p>
      <button class="reveal-btn" onclick="this.nextElementSibling.classList.toggle('show')">Show answer</button>
      <div class="answer">The near one. Since V ∝ 1/r, a smaller r gives a larger contribution to V.</div>
    </div>
  </section>

  <!-- STAGE 7 -->
  <section class="stage">
    <div class="stage-badge">7</div>
    <div class="kicker">Seeing the whole picture</div>
    <h2>Equipotential Surfaces — Contour Maps of Electric Height</h2>
    <p class="lead">If potential is like elevation, then equipotential surfaces are exactly like the contour lines on a hiking map.</p>

    <div class="box analogy">
      <span class="box-label">Analogy</span>
      On a hiking map, a contour line connects every point at the same elevation. Walking along a contour line means staying at the same height — no climbing, no descending, no work done against gravity. An <strong>equipotential surface</strong> connects every point in space that has the same electric potential. Moving a charge along it takes zero work.
    </div>

    <svg viewBox="0 0 420 220" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" style="margin:18px 0;">
      <circle cx="210" cy="110" r="4" fill="#E2792B"/>
      <circle cx="210" cy="110" r="30" fill="none" stroke="#1E8A79" stroke-width="1.6" stroke-dasharray="4 4"/>
      <circle cx="210" cy="110" r="60" fill="none" stroke="#1E8A79" stroke-width="1.6" stroke-dasharray="4 4"/>
      <circle cx="210" cy="110" r="90" fill="none" stroke="#1E8A79" stroke-width="1.6" stroke-dasharray="4 4"/>
      <g stroke="#2E6F9E" stroke-width="1.6">
        <line x1="210" y1="110" x2="210" y2="14"/>
        <line x1="210" y1="110" x2="210" y2="206"/>
        <line x1="210" y1="110" x2="114" y2="110"/>
        <line x1="210" y1="110" x2="306" y2="110"/>
        <line x1="210" y1="110" x2="146" y2="46"/>
        <line x1="210" y1="110" x2="274" y2="46"/>
        <line x1="210" y1="110" x2="146" y2="174"/>
        <line x1="210" y1="110" x2="274" y2="174"/>
      </g>
      <text x="210" y="26" text-anchor="middle" font-family="Inter" font-size="10" fill="#2E6F9E" font-weight="600">field line</text>
      <text x="330" y="107" text-anchor="middle" font-family="Inter" font-size="10" fill="#1E8A79" font-weight="600">equipotential</text>
    </svg>

    <div class="box key-idea">
      <span class="box-label">Key idea</span>
      Field lines always cross equipotential surfaces at a perfect right angle (90°). This makes sense: the field points in the direction of <em>steepest change</em> in potential — exactly like water always flows straight downhill, perpendicular to the contour lines, never along them.
    </div>
  </section>

  <!-- STAGE 8 -->
  <section class="stage">
    <div class="stage-badge">8</div>
    <div class="kicker">Before you practice</div>
    <h2>Formula Cheat Sheet</h2>
    <p class="lead">Everything from this guide, in one table. Bookmark this before starting the activity sheet.</p>

    <table class="cheat">
      <tr><th>Formula</th><th>Meaning</th></tr>
      <tr><td class="formula">Φ = E · A · cos θ</td><td>Electric flux through a surface — how much field "passes through."</td></tr>
      <tr><td class="formula">Φ<sub>total</sub> = Q<sub>enc</sub> / ε₀</td><td>Gauss's Law — total flux through any closed surface depends only on enclosed charge.</td></tr>
      <tr><td class="formula">E = kQ / r²</td><td>Electric field of a point charge (k ≈ 9×10⁹ N·m²/C²).</td></tr>
      <tr><td class="formula">V = PE / q</td><td>Electric potential — potential energy per unit charge at a point.</td></tr>
      <tr><td class="formula">V = kQ / r</td><td>Electric potential of a point charge — a scalar, no direction.</td></tr>
    </table>
  </section>

  <div class="cta">
    <h3>Ready to practice?</h3>
    <p>Grab the printable activity sheet and work through it in 75 minutes — from field lines to equipotential surfaces.</p>
    <a href="student-worksheet.html">Open the activity sheet →</a>
  </div>

    <div class="q-block">

        <div class="lines h3"></div>
    </div>

        <p><span class="qn">2.</span> A uniform field E = 400 N/C passes straight through (θ = 0°) a flat surface of area A = 1.5 m².</p>
        <div class="steps-fill">Φ = E × A = _______ × _______ = _______ N·m²/C</div>
    </div>

    <div class="q-block">

        <div class="steps-fill">Φ<sub>total</sub> = Q<sub>enc</sub> / ε₀ = _______ / _______ = _______ N·m²/C</div>
    </div>

    <div class="q-block">
        <p><span class="qn">4. (Challenge)</span> Point charge Q = 5.0 × 10⁻⁹ C sits at the centre of a spherical Gaussian surface of radius r = 0.2 m. Follow the steps to find E.</p>
        <div class="steps-fill">
          E = Q / (4πε₀r²) = kQ/r²<br>
          E = (9×10⁹ × _______) / (_______)² = _______ N/C
        </div>
    </div>
  </div>

        <p><span class="qn">1.</span> Match each electric idea to its gravity analogy by writing the matching letter.</p>
        <table class="match">
          <tr><td>Electric potential (V) &nbsp;&nbsp;<span class="blank" style="min-width:40px;"></span></td><td>A. A ball rolling downhill</td></tr>
          <tr><td>Charge moving in a field &nbsp;<span class="blank" style="min-width:40px;"></span></td><td>B. Height on a hill</td></tr>
          <tr><td>Equipotential surface &nbsp;&nbsp;<span class="blank" style="min-width:40px;"></span></td><td>C. A contour line on a map</td></tr>
        </table>
    </div>

    <div class="q-block">
        <p><span class="qn">2.</span> Find the potential V at r = 0.4 m from a point charge Q = 6 × 10⁻⁶ C. (k = 9×10⁹ N·m²/C²)</p>
        <div class="steps-fill">V = kQ/r = (_______ × _______) / _______ = _______ V</div>
    </div>

    <div class="q-block">

    </div>

  <!-- HERO -->
  <section class="hero">
    <div class="eyebrow">Electricity &amp; Magnetism · Chapter Guide</div>
    <h1>Gauss's Law &amp; <em>Electric Potential</em></h1>
    <p class="sub">Two ideas, one story: counting invisible field lines through a surface, and mapping the electric "height" that makes charges want to move. No heavy math — just patterns, pictures, and small steps.</p>

    <svg class="hero-svg" viewBox="0 0 480 300" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg">
      <circle cx="240" cy="150" r="4" fill="#E2792B"/>
      <text x="240" y="128" text-anchor="middle" font-family="JetBrains Mono" font-size="13" fill="#E2792B" font-weight="600">+Q</text>
      <g stroke="#2E6F9E" stroke-width="1.6" fill="none" opacity="0.85">
        <line x1="240" y1="150" x2="240" y2="20"/>
        <line x1="240" y1="150" x2="240" y2="280"/>
        <line x1="240" y1="150" x2="90" y2="150"/>
        <line x1="240" y1="150" x2="390" y2="150"/>
        <line x1="240" y1="150" x2="134" y2="44"/>
        <line x1="240" y1="150" x2="346" y2="44"/>
        <line x1="240" y1="150" x2="134" y2="256"/>
        <line x1="240" y1="150" x2="346" y2="256"/>
      </g>
      <g fill="#2E6F9E">
        <polygon points="240,26 235,38 245,38"/>
        <polygon points="240,274 235,262 245,262"/>
        <polygon points="96,150 108,145 108,155"/>
        <polygon points="384,150 372,145 372,155"/>
        <polygon points="139,50 148,58 138,60"/>
        <polygon points="341,50 332,58 342,60"/>
        <polygon points="139,250 148,242 138,240"/>
        <polygon points="341,250 332,242 342,240"/>
      </g>
      <circle cx="240" cy="150" r="95" fill="none" stroke="#1E8A79" stroke-width="2" stroke-dasharray="6 5"/>
      <text x="240" y="270" text-anchor="middle" font-family="Inter" font-size="12" fill="#1E8A79" font-weight="600">Gaussian surface (imaginary, closed)</text>
    </svg>
    <div class="flux-count">Field lines crossing the dashed surface: <b>8 out</b> — all counted, no matter its exact shape.</div>
  </section>

  <!-- STAGE 1 -->
  <section class="stage">
    <div class="stage-badge">1</div>
    <div class="kicker">Warm-up recap</div>
    <h2>What is an Electric Field?</h2>
    <p class="lead">Before flux and Gauss's Law can make sense, we need one picture firmly in mind: the electric field as a set of arrows filling space around a charge.</p>

    <p>An electric field is a way of describing how a charge affects the space around it, even before anything else is there. Instead of drawing forces on every possible test charge, we simplify life by drawing <strong>field lines</strong> — arrows that show the direction a small positive charge would be pushed at that point.</p>

    <div class="box key-idea">
      <span class="box-label">Three rules for field lines</span>
      1. Lines start on positive charges and end on negative charges (or head off to infinity).<br>
      2. Lines never cross each other.<br>
      3. Where lines are packed close together, the field is <em>stronger</em>. Where they spread out, the field is <em>weaker</em>.
    </div>

    <div class="check">
      <div class="qlabel">Check yourself</div>
      <p>Two diagrams show field lines around a charge. In diagram A, the lines are close together. In diagram B, the same number of lines are spread far apart. Which region has the stronger field?</p>
      <button class="reveal-btn" onclick="this.nextElementSibling.classList.toggle('show')">Show answer</button>
      <div class="answer">Diagram A. Line <em>density</em> (how packed they are) tells you field strength — not how many lines you drew in total.</div>
    </div>
  </section>

  <!-- STAGE 2 -->
  <section class="stage">
    <div class="stage-badge">2</div>
    <div class="kicker">Building the tool</div>
    <h2>Electric Flux — Counting Lines Through a Window</h2>
    <p class="lead">Flux just means: "how much field passes through a surface." Nothing more mysterious than that.</p>

    <div class="box analogy">
      <span class="box-label">Analogy</span>
      Imagine standing at an open window on a windy day. If the window faces straight into the wind, a lot of air rushes through. If you turn the window sideways to the wind, almost no air passes through — even though the wind hasn't changed at all. <strong>Electric flux</strong> works the same way, except it's field lines passing through a surface instead of wind through a window.
    </div>

    <svg viewBox="0 0 480 160" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" style="margin:18px 0;">
      <g stroke="#2E6F9E" stroke-width="1.6" fill="none">
        <line x1="20" y1="40" x2="150" y2="40"/><polygon points="150,40 140,35 140,45" fill="#2E6F9E"/>
        <line x1="20" y1="70" x2="150" y2="70"/><polygon points="150,70 140,65 140,75" fill="#2E6F9E"/>
        <line x1="20" y1="100" x2="150" y2="100"/><polygon points="150,100 140,95 140,105" fill="#2E6F9E"/>
      </g>
      <rect x="150" y="20" width="10" height="100" fill="#E4EEF6" stroke="#1E8A79" stroke-width="2"/>
      <text x="155" y="140" text-anchor="middle" font-family="Inter" font-size="11" fill="#1B2340" font-weight="600">θ = 0° (face-on)</text>
      <text x="155" y="153" text-anchor="middle" font-family="Inter" font-size="10" fill="#1E8A79">maximum flux</text>

      <g stroke="#2E6F9E" stroke-width="1.6" fill="none">
        <line x1="260" y1="40" x2="390" y2="40"/><polygon points="390,40 380,35 380,45" fill="#2E6F9E"/>
        <line x1="260" y1="70" x2="390" y2="70"/><polygon points="390,70 380,65 380,75" fill="#2E6F9E"/>
        <line x1="260" y1="100" x2="390" y2="100"/><polygon points="390,100 380,95 380,105" fill="#2E6F9E"/>
      </g>
      <rect x="373" y="55" width="34" height="10" fill="#E4EEF6" stroke="#1E8A79" stroke-width="2" transform="rotate(0 0 0)"/>
      <text x="390" y="140" text-anchor="middle" font-family="Inter" font-size="11" fill="#1B2340" font-weight="600">θ = 90° (edge-on)</text>
      <text x="390" y="153" text-anchor="middle" font-family="Inter" font-size="10" fill="#C0392B">zero flux</text>
    </svg>

    <div class="formula-card">
      <div class="flabel">General formula</div>
      <div class="feq">Φ = E · A · cos θ</div>
      <div class="fnote">Φ (phi) is flux, E is field strength, A is the surface area, and θ is the angle between the field and the surface's perpendicular ("normal") direction. When the field hits the surface straight-on, θ = 0° and cos θ = 1, so Φ = E·A — the simplest case.</div>
    </div>

    <div class="worked">
      <div class="wtitle">Worked example</div>
      <ol>
        <li>A uniform field of <span class="step-eq">E = 500 N/C</span> passes straight through a flat surface of area <span class="step-eq">A = 2 m²</span>, perpendicular to it (θ = 0°).</li>
        <li>Since θ = 0°, cos θ = 1, so we can use <span class="step-eq">Φ = E × A</span>.</li>
        <li>Φ = 500 × 2 = <span class="step-eq">1000 N·m²/C</span>.</li>
      </ol>
    </div>

    <div class="box mistake">
      <span class="box-label">Common mistake</span>
      Forgetting the cos θ term when the surface is tilted. If a problem doesn't say "perpendicular" or "face-on," check the angle before plugging numbers in.
    </div>

    <div class="check">
      <div class="qlabel">Check yourself</div>
      <p>A flat surface is turned so it's exactly edge-on to a field (θ = 90°). What is the flux through it?</p>
      <button class="reveal-btn" onclick="this.nextElementSibling.classList.toggle('show')">Show answer</button>
      <div class="answer">Zero. Since cos 90° = 0, no field lines actually pass through the surface — they slide along it instead.</div>
    </div>
  </section>

  <!-- STAGE 3 -->
  <section class="stage">
    <div class="stage-badge">3</div>
    <div class="kicker">The big idea</div>
    <h2>Gauss's Law</h2>
    <p class="lead">Gauss's Law is one simple sentence: the total flux out of any <em>closed</em> surface only depends on the charge trapped inside it.</p>

    <div class="box analogy">
      <span class="box-label">Analogy</span>
      Picture a closed fishing net wrapped all the way around part of a lake. It doesn't matter if the net is shaped like a ball, a box, or a lumpy potato — only the fish actually <em>inside</em> the net get caught. Charge outside the surface contributes nothing to the total flux, no matter how close it is.
    </div>

    <div class="formula-card">
      <div class="flabel">Gauss's Law</div>
      <div class="feq">Φ<sub style="font-size:13px;">total</sub> = Q<sub style="font-size:13px;">enc</sub> / ε₀</div>
      <div class="fnote">Φ<sub>total</sub> is the total flux through a closed surface, Q<sub>enc</sub> is the total charge enclosed inside that surface, and ε₀ (epsilon-naught) is a fixed constant, ε₀ ≈ 8.85 × 10⁻¹² C²/(N·m²).</div>
    </div>

    <div class="box key-idea">
      <span class="box-label">Key idea</span>
      The <em>shape</em> of the closed surface (called a "Gaussian surface") never matters. We are always free to imagine any convenient closed shape — usually a sphere, cylinder, or box — because Gauss's Law only cares about the charge trapped inside.
    </div>

    <div class="check">
      <div class="qlabel">Check yourself</div>
      <p>If you double the amount of charge enclosed inside a Gaussian surface, what happens to the total flux through that surface?</p>
      <button class="reveal-btn" onclick="this.nextElementSibling.classList.toggle('show')">Show answer</button>
      <div class="answer">It also doubles. Φ<sub>total</sub> = Q<sub>enc</sub>/ε₀ is a direct (linear) relationship — flux scales exactly with enclosed charge.</div>
    </div>
  </section>

  <!-- STAGE 4 -->
  <section class="stage">
    <div class="stage-badge">4</div>
    <div class="kicker">Putting it to work</div>
    <h2>Using Gauss's Law: Field of a Point Charge</h2>
    <p class="lead">Gauss's Law becomes genuinely useful when we pick a Gaussian surface with a lot of symmetry. For a single point charge, that's a sphere.</p>

    <div class="worked">
      <div class="wtitle">Step-by-step reasoning</div>
      <ol>
        <li>Draw an imaginary sphere of radius <span class="step-eq">r</span> centered on the point charge Q.</li>
        <li>By symmetry, the field E has the same strength everywhere on this sphere, and always points straight outward — perpendicular to the surface (θ = 0°, so cos θ = 1).</li>
        <li>The surface area of a sphere is <span class="step-eq">A = 4πr²</span>.</li>
        <li>So the total flux is simply <span class="step-eq">Φ = E × 4πr²</span>.</li>
        <li>Gauss's Law says this must equal <span class="step-eq">Q/ε₀</span>. Setting them equal: E × 4πr² = Q/ε₀.</li>
        <li>Solving for E: <span class="step-eq">E = Q / (4πε₀r²)</span>.</li>
      </ol>
    </div>

    <div class="formula-card">
      <div class="flabel">Field of a point charge</div>
      <div class="feq">E = kQ / r²  &nbsp; where &nbsp; k = 1/(4πε₀) ≈ 9 × 10⁹ N·m²/C²</div>
      <div class="fnote">This is the same formula you may already know from Coulomb's Law — Gauss's Law simply gives us another route to the same answer, and a much faster one for symmetric shapes.</div>
    </div>

    <div class="worked">
      <div class="wtitle">Worked example</div>
      <ol>
        <li>Find the field at r = 0.3 m from a point charge Q = 2 × 10⁻⁶ C.</li>
        <li>E = kQ/r² = (9×10⁹ × 2×10⁻⁶) / (0.3)²</li>
        <li>Numerator: 9×10⁹ × 2×10⁻⁶ = <span class="step-eq">1.8 × 10⁴</span></li>
        <li>Denominator: 0.3² = <span class="step-eq">0.09</span></li>
        <li>E = 1.8×10⁴ / 0.09 = <span class="step-eq">2 × 10⁵ N/C</span></li>
      </ol>
    </div>

    <div class="check">
      <div class="qlabel">Check yourself</div>
      <p>If you move to a point twice as far away from the same charge (r doubles), what happens to E?</p>
      <button class="reveal-btn" onclick="this.nextElementSibling.classList.toggle('show')">Show answer</button>
      <div class="answer">E becomes 4 times smaller. Because E ∝ 1/r², doubling r divides E by 2² = 4.</div>
    </div>
  </section>

  <!-- STAGE 5 -->
  <section class="stage">
    <div class="stage-badge">5</div>
    <div class="kicker">Changing viewpoint</div>
    <h2>From Field to Potential: Electric "Height"</h2>
    <p class="lead">Fields tell us about force. Potential tells us about energy — and energy is often easier to work with because it has no direction to track.</p>

    <div class="box analogy">
      <span class="box-label">Analogy</span>
      Think about a ball sitting on a hill. The higher up the hill, the more gravitational potential energy it has. Roll it, and that stored energy turns into motion. Electric charges behave the same way inside a field: a charge at a location with more electric potential energy will "roll" toward lower potential energy if it's free to move.
    </div>

    <p>Just like we divide gravitational PE by mass to get a property of the location alone (height), we divide electric potential energy by charge to get <strong>electric potential</strong> — a property of the location in the field, independent of whatever charge happens to be sitting there.</p>

    <div class="formula-card">
      <div class="flabel">Definition of electric potential</div>
      <div class="feq">V = PE / q</div>
      <div class="fnote">V is measured in volts (V), where 1 volt = 1 joule per coulomb. Potential belongs to a <em>point in space</em>, the same way elevation belongs to a spot on a map — not to whichever hiker happens to be standing there.</div>
    </div>
  </section>

  <!-- STAGE 6 -->
  <section class="stage">
    <div class="stage-badge">6</div>
    <div class="kicker">A useful formula</div>
    <h2>Potential of a Point Charge</h2>
    <p class="lead">Just like the field, the potential around a point charge follows a clean formula — but this one is simpler, because potential has no direction.</p>

    <div class="formula-card">
      <div class="flabel">Potential of a point charge</div>
      <div class="feq">V = kQ / r</div>
      <div class="fnote">Notice E ∝ 1/r² but V ∝ 1/r — potential falls off more gently with distance. And because V is a scalar (just a number, no direction), adding up the potential from several charges is simple addition — no angles required.</div>
    </div>

    <div class="worked">
      <div class="wtitle">Worked example</div>
      <ol>
        <li>Find V at r = 0.5 m from a charge Q = 3 × 10⁻⁶ C.</li>
        <li>V = kQ/r = (9×10⁹ × 3×10⁻⁶) / 0.5</li>
        <li>Numerator: 9×10⁹ × 3×10⁻⁶ = <span class="step-eq">2.7 × 10⁴</span></li>
        <li>V = 2.7×10⁴ / 0.5 = <span class="step-eq">5.4 × 10⁴ V</span></li>
      </ol>
    </div>

    <div class="check">
      <div class="qlabel">Check yourself</div>
      <p>Two point charges of equal size sit at different distances from you. Which one contributes more to the potential you experience — the near one or the far one?</p>
      <button class="reveal-btn" onclick="this.nextElementSibling.classList.toggle('show')">Show answer</button>
      <div class="answer">The near one. Since V ∝ 1/r, a smaller r gives a larger contribution to V.</div>
    </div>
  </section>

  <!-- STAGE 7 -->
  <section class="stage">
    <div class="stage-badge">7</div>
    <div class="kicker">Seeing the whole picture</div>
    <h2>Equipotential Surfaces — Contour Maps of Electric Height</h2>
    <p class="lead">If potential is like elevation, then equipotential surfaces are exactly like the contour lines on a hiking map.</p>

    <div class="box analogy">
      <span class="box-label">Analogy</span>
      On a hiking map, a contour line connects every point at the same elevation. Walking along a contour line means staying at the same height — no climbing, no descending, no work done against gravity. An <strong>equipotential surface</strong> connects every point in space that has the same electric potential. Moving a charge along it takes zero work.
    </div>

    <svg viewBox="0 0 420 220" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" style="margin:18px 0;">
      <circle cx="210" cy="110" r="4" fill="#E2792B"/>
      <circle cx="210" cy="110" r="30" fill="none" stroke="#1E8A79" stroke-width="1.6" stroke-dasharray="4 4"/>
      <circle cx="210" cy="110" r="60" fill="none" stroke="#1E8A79" stroke-width="1.6" stroke-dasharray="4 4"/>
      <circle cx="210" cy="110" r="90" fill="none" stroke="#1E8A79" stroke-width="1.6" stroke-dasharray="4 4"/>
      <g stroke="#2E6F9E" stroke-width="1.6">
        <line x1="210" y1="110" x2="210" y2="14"/>
        <line x1="210" y1="110" x2="210" y2="206"/>
        <line x1="210" y1="110" x2="114" y2="110"/>
        <line x1="210" y1="110" x2="306" y2="110"/>
        <line x1="210" y1="110" x2="146" y2="46"/>
        <line x1="210" y1="110" x2="274" y2="46"/>
        <line x1="210" y1="110" x2="146" y2="174"/>
        <line x1="210" y1="110" x2="274" y2="174"/>
      </g>
      <text x="210" y="26" text-anchor="middle" font-family="Inter" font-size="10" fill="#2E6F9E" font-weight="600">field line</text>
      <text x="330" y="107" text-anchor="middle" font-family="Inter" font-size="10" fill="#1E8A79" font-weight="600">equipotential</text>
    </svg>

    <div class="box key-idea">
      <span class="box-label">Key idea</span>
      Field lines always cross equipotential surfaces at a perfect right angle (90°). This makes sense: the field points in the direction of <em>steepest change</em> in potential — exactly like water always flows straight downhill, perpendicular to the contour lines, never along them.
    </div>
  </section>

  <!-- STAGE 8 -->
  <section class="stage">
    <div class="stage-badge">8</div>
    <div class="kicker">Before you practice</div>
    <h2>Formula Cheat Sheet</h2>
    <p class="lead">Everything from this guide, in one table. Bookmark this before starting the activity sheet.</p>

    <table class="cheat">
      <tr><th>Formula</th><th>Meaning</th></tr>
      <tr><td class="formula">Φ = E · A · cos θ</td><td>Electric flux through a surface — how much field "passes through."</td></tr>
      <tr><td class="formula">Φ<sub>total</sub> = Q<sub>enc</sub> / ε₀</td><td>Gauss's Law — total flux through any closed surface depends only on enclosed charge.</td></tr>
      <tr><td class="formula">E = kQ / r²</td><td>Electric field of a point charge (k ≈ 9×10⁹ N·m²/C²).</td></tr>
      <tr><td class="formula">V = PE / q</td><td>Electric potential — potential energy per unit charge at a point.</td></tr>
      <tr><td class="formula">V = kQ / r</td><td>Electric potential of a point charge — a scalar, no direction.</td></tr>
    </table>
  </section>

  <div class="cta">
    <h3>Ready to practice?</h3>
    <p>Grab the printable activity sheet and work through it in 75 minutes — from field lines to equipotential surfaces.</p>
    <a href="student-worksheet.html">Open the activity sheet →</a>
  </div>

        <p><span class="qn">1.</span> Look at the two diagrams below. Circle the one that shows a <strong>stronger</strong> electric field, then explain your reasoning in one sentence.</p>
        <div style="display:flex;gap:10px;justify-content:center;margin:6px 0;">
          <div class="diagram-box" style="width:48%;">
            <svg viewBox="0 0 100 80"><circle cx="50" cy="40" r="3" fill="#E2792B"/>

            </svg>
            <div style="font-size:9px;font-weight:700;">B</div>
          </div>
        </div>
        <p><span class="qn">Reason:</span></p>
        <div class="lines h1"></div>

        <p><span class="qn">2.</span> Fill in the blanks using the field-line rules from the study guide.</p>
        <p>Field lines start on <span class="blank"></span> charges and end on <span class="blank"></span> charges. Field lines never <span class="blank" style="min-width:80px;"></span> each other.</p>

        <p><span class="qn">3.</span> Draw at least 6 field lines around the positive charge below.</p>
        <div class="diagram-box" style="max-width:140px;margin:0 auto;">
          <svg viewBox="0 0 100 100"><circle cx="50" cy="50" r="4" fill="#E2792B"/><text x="50" y="70" text-anchor="middle" font-size="9" fill="#4A5578">+Q</text></svg>
        </div>
      </div>

      <div class="part">
        <div class="part-head"><h2>Part B — Gauss's Law in Action</h2><span class="mins">20 min</span></div>
        <p><span class="qn">1.</span> In your own words, explain why the <em>shape</em> of a Gaussian surface does not matter when applying Gauss's Law.</p>
        <div class="lines h2"></div>

        <p><span class="qn">2.</span> A uniform field E = 400 N/C passes straight through (θ = 0°) a flat surface of area A = 1.5 m². Find the flux Φ.</p>
        <div class="steps-fill">Φ = E × A = _______ × _______ = _______ N·m²/C</div>

        <p><span class="qn">3.</span> A closed surface encloses a charge Q<sub>enc</sub> = 4.0 × 10⁻⁹ C. Use ε₀ = 8.85 × 10⁻¹² C²/(N·m²) to find the total flux.</p>
        <div class="steps-fill">Φ<sub>total</sub> = Q<sub>enc</sub> / ε₀ = _______ / _______ = _______ N·m²/C</div>

        <p><span class="qn">4. (Challenge)</span> A point charge Q = 5.0 × 10⁻⁹ C sits at the centre of a spherical Gaussian surface of radius r = 0.2 m. Follow the steps to find E.</p>
        <div class="steps-fill">
          Φ = E × 4πr² &nbsp;&nbsp;and&nbsp;&nbsp; Φ = Q/ε₀ &nbsp;&nbsp;so&nbsp;&nbsp; E = Q / (4πε₀r²) = kQ/r²<br>
          E = (9×10⁹ × _______) / (_______)² = _______ N/C
        </div>
      </div>

    </div>

    <div class="divider"></div>

    <!-- RIGHT COLUMN -->
    <div class="col">

      <div class="part">
        <div class="part-head"><h2>Part C — Mapping Electric "Height"</h2><span class="mins">15 min</span></div>
        <p><span class="qn">1.</span> Match each electric idea to its gravity analogy by writing the matching letter.</p>
        <table style="width:100%;font-size:10.5px;border-collapse:collapse;">
          <tr><td style="padding:2px 0;">Electric potential (V) &nbsp;&nbsp;<span class="blank" style="min-width:20px;"></span></td><td style="padding:2px 0;">A. A ball rolling downhill</td></tr>
          <tr><td style="padding:2px 0;">Charge moving in a field &nbsp;<span class="blank" style="min-width:20px;"></span></td><td style="padding:2px 0;">B. Height on a hill</td></tr>
          <tr><td style="padding:2px 0;">Equipotential surface &nbsp;&nbsp;<span class="blank" style="min-width:20px;"></span></td><td style="padding:2px 0;">C. A contour line on a map</td></tr>
        </table>

        <p><span class="qn">2.</span> Find the potential V at r = 0.4 m from a point charge Q = 6 × 10⁻⁶ C. (k = 9×10⁹ N·m²/C²)</p>
        <div class="steps-fill">V = kQ/r = (_______ × _______) / _______ = _______ V</div>

        <p><span class="qn">3.</span> Point X is 0.2 m from a charge; point Y is 0.6 m from the same charge. Which point has the higher potential? Explain in one sentence.</p>
        <div class="lines h1"></div>
      </div>

      <div class="part">
        <div class="part-head"><h2>Part D — Equipotential Surfaces</h2><span class="mins">20 min</span></div>
        <p><span class="qn">1.</span> Label the diagram: write "field line" next to a straight radial line, and "equipotential" next to a dashed circle. Mark the angle where they cross.</p>
        <div class="diagram-box" style="max-width:160px;margin:0 auto;">
          <svg viewBox="0 0 120 100"><circle cx="60" cy="50" r="3" fill="#E2792B"/>
            <circle cx="60" cy="50" r="20" fill="none" stroke="#1E8A79" stroke-width="1.2" stroke-dasharray="3 3"/>
            <circle cx="60" cy="50" r="38" fill="none" stroke="#1E8A79" stroke-width="1.2" stroke-dasharray="3 3"/>
            <g stroke="#2E6F9E" stroke-width="1.2"><line x1="60" y1="50" x2="60" y2="6"/><line x1="60" y1="50" x2="103" y2="50"/><line x1="60" y1="50" x2="60" y2="94"/><line x1="60" y1="50" x2="17" y2="50"/></g>
          </svg>
        </div>

        <p><span class="qn">2.</span> True or False — circle one.</p>
        <p>a. Moving a charge along an equipotential surface takes zero work. &nbsp; <strong>T / F</strong></p>
        <p>b. Field lines are always parallel to equipotential surfaces. &nbsp; <strong>T / F</strong></p>
        <p>c. Doubling the distance from a point charge doubles its potential. &nbsp; <strong>T / F</strong></p>

        <p><span class="qn">3.</span> In 2–3 sentences, connect Gauss's Law to equipotential surfaces: how does "counting field lines" relate to "mapping electric height"?</p>
        <div class="lines h3"></div>
      </div>

    <div class="col">

      <div class="part">
        <div class="part-head"><h2>Part C — Mapping Electric "Height"</h2><span class="mins">15 min</span></div>
        <p><span class="qn">1.</span> Match each electric idea to its gravity analogy by writing the matching letter.</p>
        <table style="width:100%;font-size:10.5px;border-collapse:collapse;">
          <tr><td style="padding:2px 0;">Electric potential (V) &nbsp;&nbsp;<span class="blank" style="min-width:20px;"></span></td><td style="padding:2px 0;">A. A ball rolling downhill</td></tr>
          <tr><td style="padding:2px 0;">Charge moving in a field &nbsp;<span class="blank" style="min-width:20px;"></span></td><td style="padding:2px 0;">B. Height on a hill</td></tr>
          <tr><td style="padding:2px 0;">Equipotential surface &nbsp;&nbsp;<span class="blank" style="min-width:20px;"></span></td><td style="padding:2px 0;">C. A contour line on a map</td></tr>
        </table>

        <p><span class="qn">2.</span> Find the potential V at r = 0.4 m from a point charge Q = 6 × 10⁻⁶ C. (k = 9×10⁹ N·m²/C²)</p>
        <div class="steps-fill">V = kQ/r = (_______ × _______) / _______ = _______ V</div>

        <p><span class="qn">3.</span> Point X is 0.2 m from a charge; point Y is 0.6 m from the same charge. Which point has the higher potential? Explain in one sentence.</p>
        <div class="lines h1"></div>
      </div>

      <div class="part">
        <div class="part-head"><h2>Part D — Equipotential Surfaces</h2><span class="mins">20 min</span></div>
        <p><span class="qn">1.</span> Label the diagram: write "field line" next to a straight radial line, and "equipotential" next to a dashed circle. Mark the angle where they cross.</p>
        <div class="diagram-box" style="max-width:160px;margin:0 auto;">
          <svg viewBox="0 0 120 100"><circle cx="60" cy="50" r="3" fill="#E2792B"/>
            <circle cx="60" cy="50" r="20" fill="none" stroke="#1E8A79" stroke-width="1.2" stroke-dasharray="3 3"/>
            <circle cx="60" cy="50" r="38" fill="none" stroke="#1E8A79" stroke-width="1.2" stroke-dasharray="3 3"/>
            <g stroke="#2E6F9E" stroke-width="1.2"><line x1="60" y1="50" x2="60" y2="6"/><line x1="60" y1="50" x2="103" y2="50"/><line x1="60" y1="50" x2="60" y2="94"/><line x1="60" y1="50" x2="17" y2="50"/></g>
          </svg>
        </div>

        <p><span class="qn">2.</span> True or False — circle one.</p>
        <p>a. Moving a charge along an equipotential surface takes zero work. &nbsp; <strong>T / F</strong></p>
        <p>b. Field lines are always parallel to equipotential surfaces. &nbsp; <strong>T / F</strong></p>
        <p>c. Doubling the distance from a point charge doubles its potential. &nbsp; <strong>T / F</strong></p>

        <p><span class="qn">3.</span> In 2–3 sentences, connect Gauss's Law to equipotential surfaces: how does "counting field lines" relate to "mapping electric height"?</p>
        <div class="lines h3"></div>
      </div>

      <div class="part">
        <div class="part-head"><h2>Reflection</h2><span class="mins">10 min</span></div>

        <div class="rating"><span>Confidence — Electric potential:</span><div class="dots"><div class="dot"></div><div class="dot"></div><div class="dot"></div><div class="dot"></div><div class="dot"></div></div></div>

    <h1>Capacitor Charge-Up: Activity Sheet</h1>
    <p class="lead">Six short stages that build from concepts to calculations to real-world reasoning. Work through them in order — each one charges up your understanding a little more, just like a capacitor charging in a circuit.</p>

    <div class="info-strip">
      <div class="pill"><svg viewBox="0 0 20 20"><circle cx="10" cy="10" r="8" fill="none" stroke="#3E6E9E" stroke-width="2"/><path d="M10 5v5l3 2" stroke="#3E6E9E" stroke-width="2" fill="none"/></svg> Total duration: 75 minutes</div>
      <div class="pill"><svg viewBox="0 0 20 20"><path d="M4 4h9l3 3v9H4z" fill="none" stroke="#E85D4C" stroke-width="2"/></svg> Answer in your notebook</div>
      <div class="pill"><svg viewBox="0 0 20 20"><path d="M3 10h5l2-6 4 12 2-6h1" stroke="#4CAF8C" stroke-width="2" fill="none"/></svg> 6 stages</div>
    </div>

    <div class="instructions">
      <h3>Before you begin</h3>
      <ul>
        <li>Write your name, class, and today's date at the top of your notebook page.</li>
        <li>Answer every question with full working — final numbers alone won't earn full marks.</li>
        <li>Stuck? Use the <a href="index.html">Learning Page</a> as your reference — every stage below tells you exactly which section to revisit.</li>
        <li>Tick the checkbox next to a question once you've written the answer in your notebook, so you can track your progress on this screen.</li>
      </ul>

<main class="wrap">

  <section class="stage warmup" id="stage-warmup">
    <div class="stage-head">
      <div class="left"><div class="stage-plate">+Q</div><h2>Warm-Up — Spark Your Thinking</h2></div>
      <span class="stage-time">10 minutes</span>
    </div>
    <div class="stage-body">
      <p class="intro">No calculators yet. Just think, sketch, and predict.</p>
      <ul class="q-list">
        <li class="q-item"><input type="checkbox" class="q-check"><span class="q-text"><span class="num">1.</span>In your own words, explain what a capacitor is and why it can "store" charge even though the dielectric between its plates doesn't conduct electricity.</span></li>
        <li class="q-item"><input type="checkbox" class="q-check"><span class="q-text"><span class="num">2.</span>Sketch a simple parallel-plate capacitor. Label the two plates, the dielectric, and the sign of the charge on each plate.</span></li>
        <li class="q-item"><input type="checkbox" class="q-check"><span class="q-text"><span class="num">3.</span>Predict: if you push the plates of a capacitor closer together, what do you expect happens to its capacitance? Write your prediction and your reasoning before you check the formula in Stage 1.</span></li>
      </ul>
      <a class="ref-note" href="index.html#intro">↳ Refer to "What is a Capacitor?" on the Learning Page</a>
    </div>
  </section>

  <section class="stage define" id="stage-define">
    <div class="stage-head">
      <div class="left"><div class="stage-plate">C</div><h2>Stage 1 — Define &amp; Derive</h2></div>
      <span class="stage-time">15 minutes</span>
    </div>
    <div class="stage-body">
      
      <div class="scaffold-box">
        <div class="magic-triangle">
          <div class="mt-top">Q</div>
          <div class="mt-bottom">
            <div class="mt-left">C</div>
            <div class="mt-right">V</div>
          </div>
        </div>
        <div>
          <p><strong>Math Hint: The Magic Triangle</strong></p>
          <p>Cover the letter you want to find. If the remaining letters are top and bottom, you divide. If they are side-by-side, you multiply. <br>\( Q = C \times V \quad|\quad C = \frac{Q}{V} \quad|\quad V = \frac{Q}{C} \)</p>
        </div>
      </div>

      <p class="intro">Practice using \( C = \frac{Q}{V} \) and the parallel-plate formula.</p>
      <ul class="q-list">
        <li class="q-item"><input type="checkbox" class="q-check"><span class="q-text"><span class="num">1.</span>A capacitor stores a charge of \( 6 \times 10^{-4} \text{ C} \) when connected across a 12 V battery. Calculate its capacitance.
          <span class="given"><strong>Given:</strong> \( Q = 6 \times 10^{-4} \text{ C} \), \( V = 12 \text{ V} \rightarrow \) Find \( C \)</span>
        </span></li>
        
        <li class="q-item"><input type="checkbox" class="q-check"><span class="q-text"><span class="num">2.</span>A 470 µF capacitor is connected to a 9 V supply. How much charge does it store?
          <span class="given"><strong>Given:</strong> \( V = 9 \text{ V} \), \( C = 470 \, \mu\text{F} \)<br><em>Reminder: \( 470 \, \mu\text{F} = 470 \times 10^{-6} \text{ F} \)</em> \( \rightarrow \) Find \( Q \)</span>
        </span></li>
        
        <li class="q-item"><input type="checkbox" class="q-check"><span class="q-text"><span class="num">3.</span>A parallel-plate capacitor has plates of area \( 0.02 \text{ m}^2 \), separated by 0.5 mm of air (\( \varepsilon_r = 1 \)). Calculate its capacitance. (\( \varepsilon_0 = 8.85 \times 10^{-12} \text{ F/m} \))
          <span class="given"><strong>Given:</strong> \( A = 0.02 \text{ m}^2 \), \( d = 0.5 \text{ mm} \)<br><em>Reminder: Convert mm to m (\( 0.5 \times 10^{-3} \text{ m} \))</em> \( \rightarrow \) Find \( C \) using \( C = \frac{\varepsilon_0 \varepsilon_r A}{d} \)</span>
        </span></li>
        
        <li class="q-item"><input type="checkbox" class="q-check"><span class="q-text"><span class="num">4.</span>The same plates from question 3 are now separated by a dielectric with \( \varepsilon_r = 4 \) instead of air, at the same distance. Recalculate the capacitance, and explain in one sentence why it changed.</span></li>
        
        <li class="q-item"><input type="checkbox" class="q-check"><span class="q-text"><span class="num">5.</span>Revisit your prediction from Warm-Up question 3. Was it correct? Use the formula to justify your answer.</span></li>
      </ul>
      <a class="ref-note" href="index.html#parallel-plate">↳ Refer to "The Parallel-Plate Capacitor" on the Learning Page</a>
    </div>
  </section>

  <section class="stage circuits" id="stage-circuits">
    <div class="stage-head">
      <div class="left"><div class="stage-plate">⇄</div><h2>Stage 2 — Series &amp; Parallel Circuits</h2></div>
      <span class="stage-time">20 minutes</span>
    </div>
    <div class="stage-body">
      <p class="intro">Combine capacitors and track how charge and voltage distribute.</p>

      <div class="diagram-wrap">
        <svg viewBox="0 0 560 130" width="100%" style="max-width:520px;height:auto;">
          <!-- Series -->
          <text x="10" y="18" font-family="Space Grotesk" font-size="13" font-weight="600" fill="#3E6E9E">Series</text>

          <text x="70" y="95" font-family="IBM Plex Mono" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle">C₁=6µF</text>

          <line x1="145" y1="42" x2="145" y2="78" stroke="#3E6E9E" stroke-width="4"/>
          <line x1="155" y1="42" x2="155" y2="78" stroke="#3E6E9E" stroke-width="4"/>
          <text x="150" y="95" font-family="IBM Plex Mono" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle">C₂=3µF</text>

          <text x="115" y="20" font-family="IBM Plex Mono" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#5C6B7A">V = 18 V</text>

          <!-- Parallel Fixed Diagram -->
          <line x1="330" y1="10" x2="330" y2="18" stroke="none"/>
          <text x="330" y="18" font-family="Space Grotesk" font-size="13" font-weight="600" fill="#E85D4C">Parallel</text>

          <line x1="330" y1="45" x2="330" y2="105" stroke="#1E2A38" stroke-width="2"/>
          <line x1="470" y1="45" x2="470" y2="105" stroke="#1E2A38" stroke-width="2"/>
